BermudAir Adding Flights All Over The Place: I Don’t Know What’s Going On

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

BermudAir, the unusual boutique carrier that launched operations a few years back, keeps getting stranger. The company has just announced a massive expansion for the summer of 2027, and with this, we’ll see the airline add all kinds of seemingly random routes to and from Bermuda, plus all kinds of routes that don’t touch Bermuda.

Whether you want to fly from Nashville to Ocho Rios, or from Fredericton to Bermuda, the airline now has you covered!

For a little bit of background, in mid-2023 we saw the launch of BermudAir, a quirky niche airline with the goal of operating Embraer jets between Bermuda (BDA) and the United States and Canada. Initially the airline was going to operate all-business class planes, but it scrapped (“delayed”) that concept before launch, instead introducing a two-cabin concept (though business class just has blocked adjacent seats and better service).

As I said from the beginning, the whole thing struck me as an odd venture, essentially trying to serve Bermuda year-round profitably, while going up against the major carriers that serve the airport.

To my surprise, the airline is still in business over three years after launch, though it hasn’t necessarily been that smooth. As is pretty common for an airlines startup, the first years have been rocky, with several routes being cut after launch. For that matter, the airline faced a lawsuit over millions in unpaid airport fees at its hub, which isn’t exactly a great sign.

Several months back, BermudAir announced new winter 2026-2027 seasonal flying to places other than Bermuda. Now the airline has announced summer seasonal flying for 2027, and it’s arguably even stranger. The routes are all over the place.

In the summer of 2027, BermudAir will fly from Bermuda (BDA) to the following destinations:

On top of that, the airline will be expanding beyond Bermuda, with new seasonal services in the following markets:

Below is BermudAir’s updated map with destinations listed…

I really can’t make sense of what is going on at BermudAir. I feel like we’ve had reasons to be suspicious, when you look at the initial business model, vs. how things have played out:

I’m not suggesting there’s no demand in any of these new markets. The demand may very well be there. But there are a lot of challenges here:

What’s interesting here is how the airline announced seems to be taking a similar strategy both for this winter and for next summer. So based on the fact that the airline is moving forward with its current winter schedule, maybe this is working? I don’t know…

It’s also interesting how the airline actually got permission to operate these routes. As a reminder, Bermuda, Anguilla, and Turks & Caicos, are all British Overseas Territories, and Belize and Jamaica are former British Overseas Territory. How does the airline get permission to serve places like Honduras, though? I guess the airline is somehow getting permission to operate this as fifth freedom service, or something.

BermudAir is expanding rapidly, as the airline has announced new routes for the summer of 2027. Not only does this include several new routes to Bermuda (including out of Fredericton!), but the airline is also adding flying between the United States and Grand Cayman, Honduras, Jamaica, etc.

I think BermudAir deserves an award for the most unusual route network of any “real” airline. I certainly can’t think of any other airline in this “league.”

Francis Lawrence Signed On for One ‘Hunger Games’ Movie. Five Films Later, He’s the Keeper of Panem

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The 'Sunrise on the Reaping' director looks back on the pressure of taking over a franchise, his decade-long collaboration with Suzanne Collins, why the series endures, and where things stand on ‘Constantine 2’ and 'Red Rising.'

The morning after the New York premiere of The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping, Francis Lawrence is still taking in the view from the night before.

On one red carpet stood nearly every era of Panem. There were the newcomers: Joseph Zada, Whitney Peak and Mckenna Grace, along with franchise first-timers Kieran Culkin, Elle Fanning, Jesse Plemons, Ralph Fiennes and Glenn Close. Tom Blyth, Rachel Zegler and Josh Andrés Rivera who starred in Ballad of Songbirds & Snakes prequel, were in attendance. And there were the originals, Jennifer Lawrence, Josh Hutcherson, Woody Harrelson, Elizabeth Banks, Jena Malone and Willow Shields assembled like the franchise’s own Avengers. Related Stories Movies New 'Hunger Games' Movie on the Way? Lionsgate Film Chief Shares Latest on Franchise's Future Beyond the Book 'Percy Jackson and the Olympians': How Season 3 Brings Epic Moments to Life and Deals With Annabeth's Absence in the Books

“Last night at the premiere, for the first time, we had three generations of cast,” Lawrence tells The Hollywood Reporter of the Nov. 16 event. “There’s something really special about the time we’ve all spent together and the quality of people I’ve gotten to work with.”

Lawrence has been a constant across all of them. Since taking over from director Gary Ross with 2013’s Catching Fire, he has directed every Hunger Games film that followed, working alongside producer Nina Jacobson to turn Suzanne Collins’ dystopian saga following teenagers forced into fatal combat into one of Hollywood’s most dependable franchises. The original four films grossed roughly $2.98 billion worldwide, and Collins’ five books have sold more than 100 million copies globally, growing into one of the most successful young adult publishing franchises in history.

Still, the scale didn’t fully hit him until the final Mockingjay films. Around the release of the films, The Hunger Games: The Exhibition opened to fans, and Lawrence walked through it with his stars.

“Walking through it for the first time with Jen and Josh, and I think Liam [Hemsworth] was there, we were all just stunned that we had made something museum-worthy, with our music, our costumes, our props, pieces of our sets and the lore,” he recalls. “There’s something really special in that, knowing these stories have connected with people in a certain way.”

Amid the success, it’s easy to forget that Lawrence arrived in Panem as an outsider. He had never made a sequel to someone else’s first film, and on Catching Fire he was “joining an existing family,” with a cast “so big and so seasoned” that he felt he had a lot to prove. He also assumed it would be a one-time job.

“The weird thing is, when I signed on to Catching Fire, I thought that was going to be the only one I did,” he says.

Instead, the studio asked him to stay for the two-part Mockingjay. “A huge part of me is very appreciative of being invited to keep making these movies and be part of this team.”

That invitation, however, came with a nerve-racking stretch. A few weeks into shooting Mockingjay, production paused for a month so Lawrence and the team could promote and release Catching Fire. He would face its box office verdict with most of the next film’s shoot still ahead of him.

“Man, what if this thing bombs?” he remembers thinking of the pressure for Catching Fire success amid filming the third installment. “I now still have like 125 days of shooting with all of these people that are going to be looking at me like, ‘We just made a movie with this guy that bombed.'”

He needn’t have worried. After its 2013 release, Catching Fire became the franchise’s highest grossing film, and the once outsider became the keeper of the series. “The series was quite popular at that point, and there was a lot of excitement and confidence at the studio, so that was nice,” he says.

When Lawrence and Jacobson returned eight years later with 2023’s The Ballad of Songbirds & Snakes, the odds felt less certain. The prequel had no Katniss and centered on the rise of the tyrant Coriolanus Snow, played by Donald Sutherland in the original films with Tom Blyth taking on the role for a younger version.

“A bunch of time had gone by, and you wonder if there’s still an appetite for Hunger Games, especially Hunger Games without Katniss,” he says. “It had a very different structure, and it was a story about the person everybody hates, so a lot of things made me a little nervous.”

Sunrise on the Reaping, which traces the origins of the fan-favorite mentor Haymitch Abernathy, felt different.

“Weirdly, this one I kind of felt less pressure,” Lawrence says. “I felt much more confident that this was one that fans really wanted to see, and it felt like a return to form for movies like Catching Fire and the original.”

There being a new Hunger Games book came as a surprise to Lawrence, who assures he, along with Jacobson, are just as in the dark as everyone else. After more than a decade of working together, Lawrence and Collins have a routine. She keeps quiet while she writes, and only once her publisher confirms she has a book does she bring in Lawrence and Jacobson, always with the same modest question of whether the story is a movie. Lawrence and Jacobson always say yes.

For Sunrise, Lawrence and Jacobson got word of the novel earlier than ever, and the secrecy was tighter as a result. The manuscript was kept at Collins‘ agent’s home, and then then moved to a locked safe kept with Jacobson. Screenwriter Billy Ray wrote on an offline laptop in Jacobson’s office, while Lawrence stayed busy making secret trips to meet with Collins.

“This one we got further in advance, while she was still going back and forth with her editors,” he says. “I had read it a couple of times, Nina had read it a couple of times, and I went to New York to work with Suzanne on breaking down the book into what we thought the movie would be. We hired [original screenwriter] Billy Ray. He was already writing and we were having calls about the draft. So a lot of work was done before the book was even announced, and I couldn’t talk to anybody about it.”

The book and its film adaptation were announced together in June 2024, which brought “a lot of relief,” he says. “At least then people knew we were working on it and that it was the Haymitch story. We just had to keep quiet about the storyline itself.”

That left the biggest challenge: finding a young Haymitch, the carefree kid from District 12 long before he became the drunken, broken mentor Harrelson made iconic. The search led to Zada, the breakout from Prime Video’s We Were Liars and Netflix’s East of Eden. Lawrence put him “through the wringer” in auditions before landing on the actor to carry the beloved character’s origin story. The seeds for the origin story were “planted over twenty years ago” when Collins began the original trilogy, the author reflected in a letter shared on social media amid the release of Sunrise.

Collins praised Zada’s performance as “remarkably powerful and moving.”

Long before he was steering a multibillion-dollar franchise, Lawrence spent years making some of the most memorable music videos of the MTV era for artists like Britney Spears, Lady Gaga, Justin Timberlake and Jennifer Lopez. He doesn’t think much about those days when he’s making a movie, he says, but their influence is still there nearly 20 years later.

“Having done maybe around 120 videos, it gave me a playground to learn the toolkit I have for making things look a certain way,” Lawrence says. “I got to create different worlds, try different things, learn about visual effects and learn about working with performers. So in terms of visual style, I just had confidence in the image in general: if I imagine something, how can I actually make that happen? Because of videos, that became second nature, so I could focus more on performance and story. That’s probably the biggest way it’s affected my moviemaking in general.”

It’s fitting, then, that music runs through Panem. The films have long used it to amplify their themes of resistance. Jennifer Lawrence’s Katniss sings “The Hanging Tree,” which became a rebel anthem. In the prequels, Zegler’s Lucy Gray Baird and Peak’s Lenore Dove are both musicians who capture the hearts of Snow and Haymitch.

Lawrence approaches that music the way he approached his videos: “much more music-forward than lyrics-forward.”

“I take the mood, tone and feel of a song, and most of the ideas I built for my videos were based on the mood of the song rather than the lyrics. Not always, but I’d say 90 percent of the time. It’s the same here: I’m primarily thinking about the mood, tone and emotional value of music and what I want something to feel like,” he explains.

Music is also common ground with Collins. “She’s a real music aficionado with a library in her head, and I had long conversations with her and with [executive music producer and songwriter] Dave Cobb about the creation of songs. She [Collins] usually has a historical background for why and how she wrote certain things, and I would talk to Dave, our music producer, mostly about how I wanted songs to feel. So whether it’s ‘The Hanging Tree,’ a Covey song or ‘The Old Therebefore,’ it’s really about mood,” he says.

The music doesn’t stop when the cameras roll. “I also listen to a lot of music when I shoot,” Lawrence says. “Often I’ll be watching a scene with music I have as inspiration playing in my ear, whether it’s score from our movies or from other movies, because I’m evoking a mood, a tone and an emotional value I want to feel in the scene. So sometimes I’m listening to dialogue and to music on my phone at the same time.”

His usual choices: The franchise’s scores, of which there are five movies worth, and “Lily’s Theme,” Alexandre Desplat’s piece from Harry Potter and the Deathly Hallows – Part 2.

“That has always felt very Hunger Games-ish to me,” he says of the Potter score. “I also listened to some more disturbing stuff for this one, because I thought of it as a bit scarier. Instead of listening to action-oriented music during fight sequences, I’d listen to things that are a little more disturbing and scary. So it was kind of all over the place.“

Building worlds became Lawrence’s calling card when he made the leap to features. His 2005 debut, Constantine, was a dark take on the DC Comics antihero, and he followed it by envisioning a New York City post-plague for the Will Smith hit I Am Legend (2007). Then came the Great Depression-era romance Water for Elephants (2011), a reunion with Jennifer Lawrence on the spy thriller Red Sparrow (2018), and more recently Slumberland (2022) and the Stephen King adaptation The Long Walk (2025).

The genres vary, but in hindsight, the dystopian streak running through several of them looks a lot like a rehearsal for the road to the Panem Capitol. Amid revisiting Hunger Games in the prequels, the director says he’s “trying to” return to where it all began with his first feature, Constantine. It was announced in 2022 that Lawrence would helm a sequel with star Keanu Reeves reprising his role and Akiva Goldsman writing the script. “We have a script,” Lawrence confirms.

Part of the appeal of a sequel is getting the band back together. The other part is unfinished business with the ratings board, and this time Lawrence wants to lean into the R rating he once tried to avoid.

“We got saddled with an R rating even though we followed all the PG-13 rules, and that was a tough blow,” he says. “On Hunger Games, every time I turn in a cut, it’s an R, we go back and forth a little, and we get to PG-13. With Constantine, they said, ‘We’ve stopped taking notes. There’s no getting a PG-13 on this movie.’ So we were stuck. Part of it was: let’s make another one because we enjoyed it, but this time let’s make the hard-R version. If we’re going to get an R, let’s make the R-rated Constantine.”

However, the road to a sequel has been anything but smooth.

“We’ve been trying, and we’ve hit roadblock after roadblock,” Lawrence says. “The character was given to somebody to make a network television show, which I never watched, and I don’t think it lasted long.” He continues, “Then J.J. [Abrams] somehow had the rights, or control of the character, for a while and was going to do something with Constantine. But he switched over, and he’s now involved in our project too. He’s a great guy, and he’s pro making this Constantine movie, which is fantastic.”.

The other obstacle, Lawrence says, is “there were new people running DC with their own plans for the characters.”

Though he doesn’t name anyone, Lawrence notes there has since been a regime change at DC. It was announced on Nov. 9 that James Gunn and Peter Safran are stepping down as co-CEOs of DC studios four years after taking the job. Skydance, which now oversees the division following the acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery, has appointed former Marvel exec Jeremy Latcham to the post of president and chief creative officer of DC Studios.

With new leadership in place, and with relationships of his own among the executives now running Warner Bros., Lawrence sees an opening. “Maybe there’s some renewed hope to get Constantine through,” he says.

“I’m a believer that we could make a sequel to our Constantine with Keanu, and if people at DC wanted to do a different version of Constantine in some sort of shared universe, they could do that, and the two could coexist.”

Constantine isn’t the only long-gestating project on his plate. With Sunrise now in theaters, Lawrence is also turning his attention to Red Rising, Pierce Brown’s bestselling sci-fi book series, which he has been developing with the author, a friend, for about eight years.

Development of an adaptation first began as a series at Netflix, with two pilot scripts he calls “fantastic,” but it stalled. “There’s just regime changes, and it was an expensive buy-in for them,” he says. “We kept getting shifted around to different departments.”

“They never really pulled the trigger,” he adds. “Now there’s a renewed excitement because we’re at Paramount and it’s theatrical again. We have a screenwriter working on it, so I’m very excited.”

All roads seemingly lead back to Panem, with Lawrence acknowledging how Red Rising can have a clear thread back to Hunger Games character Plutarch Heavensbee, the Gamemaker turned rebel played by Plemons in Sunrise and the late Philip Seymour Hoffman in the original films. “One of the things that I love about Plutarch is that Plutarch is a bit of an infiltrator,” Lawrence says. Red Rising is an infiltration story too. The story follows Darrow, described as “a rebel forged by tragedy, battling to lead his oppressed people to freedom.”

“It allows characters to really question themselves, cross weird moral boundaries and question the people around them. What happens when the people you hate suddenly become more relatable than some of the people that you’re fighting for?” he says. “I’ve always been fascinated by those kinds of stories.”

Also on deck is BioShock, an adaptation of 2K Games’ 2007 first-person shooter. “We’re doing a rewrite on a script now,” Lawrence says. “I think that’s getting closer and closer.” In fact, he had a call about the project scheduled for shortly after his conversation with THR.

Thirteen years after the Hunger Games franchise began, the odds remain firmly in Panem’s favor. In its opening weekend, Sunrise on the Reaping grossed $120 million, becoming its fourth-best debut in franchise history, besting 2015’s second Mockingjay movie ($102.6 million) and 2023 prequel The Ballad of Songbirds & Snakes ($44.6 million) but behind 2013’s Catching Fire ($158 million), 2012’s series-launching The Hunger Games ($152.5 million) and 2014’s first Mockingjay ($121.8 million).

For Lawrence, who has lived in this world for more than a decade, the prequels have done more than extend the franchise. They’ve changed how he understands it.

“Going through each of these books page by page with Suzanne over time has given me a pretty deep knowledge of the world and the lore. But now that she’s writing these prequels and reframing characters, I’m also learning more each time she releases a book,” he says.

He counts himself a fan, but perhaps not the biggest one on set. “I’m sure there are super fans, even people like Mckenna Grace [Maysilee Donner in Sunrise], who is a super fan of these stories, who know more about all the little nooks and crannies than I do,” he says with a laugh.

After so many years of working side by side with Collins, the page and the screen have started to merge in his mind. “The lines between the book and the script start to blur, and you forget what’s just in the movies and what’s exactly from the book,” he says.

Plenty of dystopian YA series followed in The Hunger Games‘ wake, Divergent and The Maze Runner among them, but none has lasted the way Panem has. Lawrence credits Collins’ finger on the pulse of the world as the reason for the story maintaining its popularity.

“Part of what originally separated these books from other YA publications: they had real meat on the bone thematically,” Lawrence says of the story’s lasting appeal. “She also created a world, a narrative and characters people fell in love with: Katniss, Peeta, Effie Trinket. But if they didn’t have the ideas underneath them, they would feel a bit empty and surface-level, and they wouldn’t land the same way. That’s one of the things I feel really lucky about, honestly: we get to make movies that lots of people go to see, but that really have something to say about the world we live in. For me, that’s why they endure.”

Does telling this story in today’s climate feel different than it did when the original films came out? “A little bit,” Lawrence says. But the ideas, he argues, have always been built to last.

“Suzanne’s original inspiration was watching war footage, then switching channels and seeing reality game shows, and she decided to write this series about the consequences of war for teens. At the time, it was about an idea that was relevant in the moment but also timeless. That’s what she does: she looks at the world and finds ideas that are relevant but weirdly still timeless,” he says.

Whether Collins has more to say in Panem is a question only she can answer. In the meantime, the world she built keeps showing up in the real one. Fans dress as the characters at premieres, Lawrence notes, and the films’ songs and three-finger salute have even appeared at protests, “whether it’s Hungary, whether it’s Paris.”

“So much of what is created is so disposable,” he says, “and I feel like luckily Hunger Games is not.”

MLB CBA negotiations timeline: League, MLBPA trade new proposals over revenue sharing as Dec. 1 deadline nears

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

If not listed, please contact your TV provider.

As the 2026 Major League Baseball offseason continues to unfold, something vitally important to the future of the sport quietly hums in the background: the ongoing negotiations over a new collective bargaining agreement.

The collective bargaining agreement, or CBA (or Basic Agreement, as it's known in a formal sense), is the negotiated accord between players and clubs -- i.e., the team owners and commissioner Rob Manfred, who represents their interests -- that governs the working relationship between the two parties, typically for a period of five years. It covers things as mundane as players' meal allowances and travel protocols and as vital as minimum salaries, the structure of free agency, revenue-sharing specifics, and roster sizes. Every CBA can be distilled down to a fight over money, or, more specifically, how the league's revenues will be divided between players and team owners.

The first CBA covered the 1968 and 1969 seasons and was the first in professional sports history. That was the handiwork of Marvin Miller, the pioneering head of the Players Association (i.e., the players union) and belated Hall of Famer. It was Miller's organizing skills, foresight, and training as a union economist that allowed him to make the Players Association a viable and effective labor organization. Every CBA negotiation from 1972 through 1994-95 resulted in some form and scale of labor stoppage, whether a players' strike or an owner-implemented lockout. After the cancellation of the 1994 World Series because of the worst labor stoppage in the sport's history, a long run of peace followed. That peace lasted until the prior CBA negotiations, which resulted in a 99-day lockout forced by team owners and delayed the start of the 2022 season.

The current CBA negotiations have been underway for some time, and not surprisingly, the two sides remain far apart on several key issues. The biggest fight will be over the owners' renewed desire to implement a salary cap, which the union continues to view as a non-starter. The push for a cap has less to do with the notions of competitive balance and more to do with the ownership's internal belief that being the only non-salary-capped league among the major North American sports is harming franchise values. The cap, though, is but one of many pressing issues on which players and owners must find congruity.

Moving forward, the process will be uncertain and rife with posturing and pointed public comments from both sides. The hope, though, is that a new CBA will be agreed to before the middle of March 2027, at which point there's a high risk of having the 2027 season delayed or even abbreviated.

To keep you informed of the twists and turns along the way to a new CBA, we've provided a detailed timeline of negotiations. That timeline will illuminate the important and finer points of the talks and, along the way, explain the implications of the various proposals coming from each side. The hope, of course, is that all of it culminates in a timely agreement that permits a fully intact and on-time 2027 season.

In earlier iterations of negotiations, MLB proposed a salary-escrow system like the ones found in the NBA and NHL. This would mean a percentage of player salaries is withheld in case league revenue projections fall short for a given year. If that's the case, then that money in escrow goes back to the league to cover the revenue shortfalls. If revenues are higher than projected, that excess is shared with the players. The MLBPA has pointedly opposed the escrow system on the grounds that it undermines the notion of guaranteed player salaries. In its latest statement on the negotiations, MLB withdrew the escrow proposal. "For months, union leadership has attacked the escrow provision used in other sports and mischaracterized the issue as the end of guaranteed contracts," MLB spokesperson Glen Caplin said in a statement. "MLB took another step today by withdrawing our escrow proposal, removing any doubt that every single guaranteed player contract will remain fully guaranteed in the new salary cap and floor system."

The specifics of the new proposal, which like all other league proposals thus far been tied to the players' accceptance of a cap and floor on team payrolls:

In response, interim union head Bruce Meyer released the following statement:

"MLB today modified its salary cap proposal to 'eliminate' escrow, but once again it provides no real benefit under their proposed cap system. Instead of holding back player money each year, MLB's new proposal is to take that same amount of money out of players' pockets in the following year. It's a different way of achieving the same objective of the salary cap - to reduce and suppress player salaries and freedom of movement. This is yet another example of how MLB's salary cap proposal pits player against player, past, present, and future."

In their latest bargaining session, the MLBPA maintained its previous proposals aimed to increase revenue sharing support for small market teams, including a mechanism designed to punish teams that do not put revenue sharing funds into the roster. The union also said the proposal is designed to remove "any league excuses for the continuation of blackouts."

Here are the details of the union's latest proposal:

As for MLB's proposal, it now includes a $1 million minimum salary (previous proposals were described as $1 million but not quite that amount), and a new series of bonuses for all player classes:

MLB's proposal is once again tied to the MLBPA's acceptance of a salary cap/floor system, which the players association has insisted it will never allow.

"The league's proposals today don't add a single dollar in overall player compensation as compared to prior proposals, and once again are expressly conditioned on accepting their salary cap and other anti-player restrictions," MLBPA executive director Bruce Meyer said in a statement. "Any supposed benefits to certain classes of players are simply taken out of the pockets of other players and are vastly outweighed by the negative effects of the cap and MLB's other restrictions. These negative effects include, but are not limited to, a drastic reduction in overall player compensation and benefits, the end of fully guaranteed contracts, and dramatic restrictions on players' career choices and freedom to contract."

The MLBPA on Friday put forth a series of proposed changes to the international signing structure. Notably, the union did not propose the implementation of an international draft, which the league has long sought. "We are committed to negotiating a system that preserves opportunities for future generations of players, protects young prospects from exploitation, and provides flexibility without further burdening international players with new artificial restrictions like a draft," MLBPA interim leader Bruce Meyer said in a statement released by the union. "Our proposals help build upon baseball's advances as an international game, while safeguarding the interests of young athletes who are the future of our sport."

Specifically, the union's proposal calls for:

Also on Friday, MLB removed from the bargaining table an earlier proposal to exclude high school players from the annual MLB Draft. MLB spokesperson Glen Caplin said the following in a statement released by the league, which also includes a call for the implementation of an international draft:

"In recognition of the feedback we received from the MLBPA on our domestic amateur proposal, we made a concession today by withdrawing our proposal on high school eligibility and proposed to maintain the current entry rules for domestic amateurs. On the international system, we continue to believe it is long past time to reform the system in ways that would address longstanding challenges and benefit future players. The enhanced transparency of the International Draft that we have proposed best addresses the root causes of corruption in the current system."

Speaking to the media at the owners meetings in New York, MLB commissioner Rob Manfred said while there's room for compromise in general with the MLBPA, there's no room for compromise on the proposed cap and floor on team payrolls. "The owners want a deal with a salary cap and floor," he said.

"I can't imagine the owners ratifying a deal that does not include a cap and floor," Manfred also said. "... Thirty clubs are 100 percent unanimous in support of the proposals that have been made. I'm not in a position to speculate about what happens in the event that we don't achieve our overriding objective, which is to get a cap and floor system that addresses the competitive problem that our fans perceive."

MLBPA interim head Bruce Meyer, who has insisted that the union will never accept a salary cap, pushed back again.

"There can be no clearer example of the league's lack of seriousness than their insistence that they will not negotiate over the economics outside of a radical change to the system that would dramatically reduce overall player compensation for all categories of players and strip fundamental rights from players like the ability to have truly guaranteed contracts," he said Wednesday in a statement.

"There should be no illusions about the owners' true motivation in pushing for this system. They want to make more money in profits and franchise values by artificially suppressing player salaries."

To date, the league has tied every individual proposal to the players' acceptance of a cap-and-floor system. The players through their union have historically shown no willingness to discuss a cap on payrolls.

MLB Players Association executive director Bruce Meyer said MLB is not serious about preventing the lockout and has "wasted months of time" trying to court public opinion when the two sides could have been having substantive negotiations.

"At the end of the day, this is going to come down to the bargaining table," Meyer told the Boston Globe. "And we have been for months, since May, looking for a serious partner in negotiations, and the league has insisted since May that they won't negotiate over anything economic outside of our agreement to a salary cap. In that respect, we've wasted months of time when we could have been having a serious negotiation. So I'd say the more the league is focused on things other than bargaining with us in good faith, I would say that's counterproductive."

Meyer also said the MLBPA has put forth proposals intended to promote competitive balance and correct the game's revenue disparity, but the owners are focused on a salary cap to "increase their profits and franchise values." He added that the two sides have made progress on non-economic matters, such as drug testing and gambling policies.

To allow for a "smoother transition" to a salary cap/floor system, MLB proposed delaying its implementation until 2028. That would give teams time to prepare and get their payrolls in order, and allow for a full offseason heading into the system. The current competitive balance tax and revenue sharing system would remain in place in 2027.

"We recognize that clubs will need a full offseason to prepare to operate within the cap and floor system. Therefore, with this offseason already underway, we proposed today to begin the cap and floor system in 2028," MLB spokesman Glen Caplin said in a statement. "This proposal would allow a smoother transition into the cap and floor system."

MLB also upped its salary cap/floor proposal to $258.3 million and $180.2 million, respectively. Back in June, the league proposed a $245.3 million cap and $171.2 million floor. It says it is willing to discuss further phase-in scenarios for a salary cap/floor system.

In its latest proposal, MLB proposed reducing the regular season from 162 games to 154 games, and adding a standard off day to every week. The league also proposed extending the Division Series from a best-of-five to a best-of-seven, and allowing higher seeded teams to select their opponents in the Wild Card Series and Division Series. As with every MLB proposal to date, the 154-game schedule and changes to the postseason format are contingent on the MLBPA accepting a salary car/floor system.

If labor strife lops off 60 or more games from the 2027 MLB season, then the consequences could be significant. The Athletic recently reported that if the forthcoming lockout extends into late May or so and costs at least 60 games, then most of the league's national broadcast and streaming contracts would be extended for another year, through 2029. That's significant because commissioner Rob Manfred hopes to bundle and centralize team streaming rights and share those revenues equally, and the current timeline would theoretically allow that to happen at the same time the national contracts are up for renegotiation. Tacking on another year to those national contracts would push back the significant windfall that owners and the league are expecting from that new media rights structure. As such, this is a heavy incentive, at least on the league side, to not let any labor stoppage drag into June.

A poll of current MLB players across the full spectrum of salaries and service time conducted by ESPN showed strong resistance to the payroll cap and floor being demanded by ownership and league and being put forth as a precondition to every proposed change from that side. An excerpt:

How open are you to a salary cap?NL starting pitcher entering arbitration: Not open.

NL infielder making eight figures annually: Zero. No chance.

NL infielder on two-year contract: Zero. Absolute zero.

NL pre-arbitration journeyman: From what I've been told, I don't think it's good for the game. I just don't know if I know all the pros and cons of it. But, from everything we've been told, it doesn't sound like it's a good thing.

NL reliever on multiyear contract: With the right criteria, I'd be open to it.

AL starting pitcher in arbitration: I don't want it. In '94, they canceled the World Series, so if those guys can miss over half a year and miss the World Series, I think we're fully prepared to hold out for sure.

AL catcher entering arbitration: I need more information, but perhaps I would listen on it.

That's mostly strong sentiment that aligns with the MLBPA position on the cap, but it's not ironclad. MLB in negotiations and its public statements is seeking to tap into any existing divide between, broadly speaking, lower-salaried players with not much service time and more tenured veterans in their higher-earning years. There is a bit of a schism there, but it would take a substantial one to shift, in any exploitable way, the union's long-standing opposition to a cap-and-floor system. The ESPN piece covers an array of CBA topics. With the two questions related to the proposed cap, the responses can be broken down as 16 in mostly firm opposition to a cap and five not necessarily in favor but open to further discussions and reflection.

MLB proposed a series of changes to current roster rules that, in a statement, the league says are aimed at "creating more stability in rosters, getting the best players to the Major Leagues more quickly, and accelerating the pace of the offseason." Among the rule changes included in MLB's proposal are:

All the proposed roster rule changes are contingent on the MLB Players Association agreeing to a salary cap-and-floor system.

During a negotiating session with the league, the players' union puts forth its own proposed changes to the MLB Draft -- changes that are, in essence, a rejoinder to the drastic overhaul earlier put forward by MLB. Among the changes the MLBPA proposes are:

Additionally, the union proposes increasing the money payable to draftees under the incentive bonus plan and eliminating, according to the MLBPA, "the current practice of clubs deducting certain bonuses from players' continuing education benefits."

The timing of the draft has actually been a concern for front offices for years, since it was shifted later in the calendar to coincide with the All-Star break in 2021. Holding the draft in July crams both that and the trade deadline into just a few weeks and making it more difficult to allocate resources.

Commissioner Rob Manfred and interim union head Bruce Meyer each hold press conferences on the day of the All-Star Game in Philadelphia. "I think that I have an ownership group that is more united than any group in my entire time of baseball," Manfred says, referring at least obliquely to the league's push for a salary cap. "They are a group that believes in what I am arguing for."

"The supposed stewards of the game have spent an inordinate amount of time trying to convince those same fans that they don't have hope or they shouldn't have hope or that the product that they're paying to consume in record numbers is somehow broken," Meyer says of MLB's efforts to highlight perceived competitive-balance issues in the face of increasing attendance and television ratings. "I think it's perverse."

The MLBPA puts forth multiple proposals, many of them dealing with procedural matters for purposes of player health, safety, and wellness. They include:

The union's latest proposal is a reminder of the breadth of CBA discussions. Most issues are not the big-ticket items like a salary cap or lack thereof or revenue sharing structure but instead are somewhat esoteric matters that strike most fans as mundane. To players and teams, though, they're important considerations and in some instances may even provide a foundation of common ground moving forward.

More face-to-face negotiations take place, and MLB proposes more changes to the free-agency system, among other asks. MLB's latest proposal includes the following, all of which depend upon the union's acceptance of a payroll cap:

MLB in a prior proposal called for a cap on team payrolls of $245.3 million starting in 2027 and a payroll floor of $171.2 million. The league issues a statement that reads in full:

"The biggest issue baseball fans want solved to strengthen the game is fixing the payroll disparity that leaves too many fans without hope of their team competing for a World Series title. Every other major U.S. sport has tackled this problem, and every year more small market teams in those leagues have a chance to win. The salary cap and floor proposal levels the playing field, allowing us greater flexibility to address longstanding player priorities while sharing baseball revenue with the players 50/50.

"Today, in addition to proposing the largest ever increase in the minimum salary, earned by over half of MLB players, we accepted two landmark changes to free agency that have been in place for 50 years. We agreed to both the MLBPA's proposal to provide earlier access to free agency, and their proposal to eliminate the qualifying offer system, a provision players view as a drag on free agency. We also proposed to eliminate deferred compensation and to create a new 'Cornerstone Player' provision similar to the NBA's 'Bird Rights' to give every team a fair shot at retaining their fans' favorite star players.

"We will continue working with the MLBPA during the bargaining process to improve the game for teams, players and fans."

"After making a series of proposals to reduce player compensation by billions of dollars, eliminate fundamental rights with a salary cap, and destroy the amateur entry process, Major League Baseball and team owners are now attempting to distract from the true impact their plan would have on baseball. These misleading offers are designed to look like 'improvements' but are of little or no value, given they are expressly conditioned on agreement to the league's cap system which eliminates the free market, and ensures gains for one player only come at the expense of another.

"The league also introduced a litany of additional restrictions on player rights — limiting salaries, contract length, performance, award, and signing bonuses. While MLB claims to be acting in the interest of fans, their proposals thus far are entirely consistent with owners' long-held goals: suppressing player salaries and maximizing club profits.

"Owners' attempts to pit players against players are nothing new, but they've failed in the past and will fail again now, because PA members remain unified. We are committed to achieving a fair deal that protects the rights of all players, promotes competition, and leaves our game better for future generations."

Lacking from the league's latest proposal, at least on the public-facing side, is any revisiting of the recent changes to the draft and any mention of prior proposed changes to the league's revenue-sharing system.

During these same face-to-face sessions, the MLBPA proposes a ban on prop bets placed on individual players, ESPN reports. The idea is to preempt harrassment of players by gamblers. The proposed ban would extend to bets placed on individual player performance prior to and during any MLB game. The MLBPA also requested that players be permitted to engage in paid endorsements and sponsorships with sportsbooks and prediction markets.

MLB and the MLBPA meet for face-to-face negotiations, and owners propose sweeping changes to the draft and also push for an international draft. As for the MLB Draft, formally known as the First-Year Player Draft, MLB pushes for the following changes:

"Over the last several years, college baseball has undergone a remarkable transformation," MLB says in a statement. "Expanded scholarships, NIL opportunities, revenue sharing and significant investments in facilities and player development have made college baseball an increasingly important pathway that is producing major league-ready talent at an accelerated rate. Today's top programs provide players with resources, competition and national exposure that were unimaginable a decade ago. Our proposal is designed to build on that momentum to benefit the game at the college, minor-league and major-league levels. By creating a draft system centered around college-aged players and making most college players eligible one year earlier, more players will benefit from both a college education and an elite development environment while reaching professional baseball -- and ultimately the major leagues -- more quickly. We believe these changes will strengthen college baseball and deepen fans' connection to the next generation of major league stars. We look forward to working with the MLBPA throughout the bargaining process to modernize the domestic amateur system in a way that benefits players, clubs, and fans."

As for the international draft, MLB proposed making players outside of the U.S., Puerto Rico, and Canada (who are already included in the standard draft) subject to a similar system. The international draft would, as with the proposed changes to the MLB Draft, come with a $200 million bonus cap. At present, international amateurs may sign with any team under the international free agent system, although teams do have hard budget caps each year.

The MLBPA also issues a statement in response to MLB's proposals:

Today, MLB made another set of proposals that are flat out bad for baseball, ones that would cripple the next generation of players and damage the future of our game. They would, among other things:

Players remain committed to bargaining in good faith and leaving baseball better than they found it -- the league's proposals fall woefully short.

U.S. President Donald J. Trump publicly supports MLB's efforts to implement a salary cap. "If you don't have a salary cap, you don't have a sport, because they can't help themselves," Trump says. "Football has a salary cap. They should have done it a long time ago.

"It's shocking, frankly, that they didn't put a cap on many years ago," he said, possibly referencing the 1994-95 strike. "They had a chance to do a cap, but they blew it."

Speaking to reporters following the owners' quarterly meetings, Manfred addresses the luxury-tax system he and the owners seek to replace with a cap. "We have tried mightily over several rounds of bargaining to use a competitive balance (luxury) tax to address competitive concerns," Manfred says. "And sometimes you've got to admit you failed.

"We made a proposal on one set of topics. At the outset of negotiations, I went and said myself, 'We're open to whatever ideas people have, but we need a realistic framework that addresses the fans' concerns about competitive balance.' You just can't ignore that financial penalties have not gotten it done for us."

In a call with reporters, MLBPA interim executive director Bruce Meyer addresses the owners' proposals. "Using MLB's definition of revenue and player share as set forth in their proposal and their presentation to us, player share under their proposal would go down," Meyer says. "Player share for this season, 2026, is projected to be well over 50 percent, using, again MLB's definitions of revenues and what counts against player share.

"Had MLB's proposal been in place in 2026, players, we estimate, would lose over half a billion dollars."

Meyer also refers to a salary cap as "a form of institutionalized collusion."

"Our salary cap-and-floor proposal addresses our fans' concerns by leveling the playing field while sharing baseball revenue with the players 50-50 like the other leagues," MLB spokesperson Glen Caplin responded on behalf of the league. "Under our proposal, major-league players will receive more compensation in year one of the system than in 2026. We are ready to listen if the MLBPA wants to counter our proposal at the bargaining table."

MLB and the owners publicly announce their first formal CBA proposal. Chiefly, they propose:

Using competitive balance tax (CBT), or luxury tax, payrolls as a guide, since they include benefits expenses, nine teams at present would be above the proposed cap line, while 12 teams would be below the floor. However, as Craig Goldstein points out, the amount needed to get all these teams below or above the relevant payroll thresholds amounts to an $18.7 million loss in aggregate player salary overall. Another sticking point will be how league revenues are defined. Owners no doubt seek to carve out things like real estate developments surrounding ballparks and ownership stakes in regional sports. As well, MLB's working definition of league revenues includes many expense deductions.

On another level, the fact that Manfred is able to persuade large-market, high-revenue clubs to agree to equal sharing of all local revenues is a notable development. However, such a concession by those large-market clubs may be contingent upon the implementation of a cap.

Another sticking point is that the league proposes an escrow system, as is used in the NBA and the NHL. This would mean a percentage of player salaries is withheld. If revenues fall short of projections, some of that money in escrow goes back to the league. If revenues are higher than projected, that excess is shared with the players. The MLBPA opposes the escrow system on the grounds that it undermines the notion of guaranteed player salaries.

The MLBPA makes an opening suite of proposals to the league, including:

"Today, the MLBPA (Major League Baseball Players Association) presented a comprehensive set of economic proposals designed to advance the rights and benefits of players at all levels," Meyer says in a statement released by the union. "Our goal is to preserve and improve baseball's market system, rewarding competition on and off the field. Additionally, the players' proposals provide increased revenue sharing initially guaranteeing every small market club a minimum of $240m in revenue every season. This enhanced revenue sharing includes added protections to ensure clubs prioritize winning over profiteering. Ultimately, our proposals are designed to build upon the incredible momentum and popularity of our sport world-wide."

"We appreciate the union making a set of proposals and we look forward to continuing the bargaining process and working towards solving the competitive balance problem our fans are telling us needs to be addressed," Caplin says in a statement. "We understand their proposals are designed to benefit players. Unfortunately, they do not address and in fact exacerbate the competitive balance problem our fans are telling us we must address. The MLBPA's proposal would reduce the amount transferred to lower-revenue Clubs, weaken the Competitive Balance Tax, and lead to even more payroll disparity than exists today. For example, under the Union's proposal, the Dodgers would pay less in luxury tax payments, giving them an additional $70 million to spend on payroll."

Negotiations on a new CBA formally begin between teams and players. Among the key issues to be discussed will be the league's desire for a salary cap, drastic changes to the revenue sharing system, league expansion, possible further playoff expansion, and an international draft.

The 2022-26 Basic Agreement is set to expire at 11:59 p.m. ET, at which point owners are expected to lock out the players in the absence of a new agreement.

How Netflix and the NFL Hope to Establish New Thanksgiving Eve Tradition With Game and U2 Half-Time Show: ‘We Want to Create Those Global Water Cooler Moments’

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Thanksgiving Day has sported NFL games since the league’s inception in 1920, and now Turkey Day tussles on the gridiron are supercharged for the streaming future.

On Nov. 25, Netflix and the NFL would like football fans to join them for a new holiday tradition: the Thanksgiving Eve game. And like the streamer’s Christmas Day gridiron matchups, this one is A-list all the way: the Los Angeles Rams vs. the Green Bay Packers at L.A.’s SoFi Stadium and a halftime show headlined by U2. Related Stories ‘PAW Patrol’ Spinoff Series ‘Skye’s Pup Squad’ Set for 2027 Release

“As the NFL opened up more inventory and started talking to us about [possibly adding] more games, [Thanksgiving Eve] is one that they mentioned and immediately it felt like the perfect fit,” says Spitzer, who spoke with Variety while sporting a New England Patriots cap.

Onnie Bose, the NFL’s VP of broadcasting, echoes that sentiment. The league has learned “over the last few years that when family and friends are together, they gravitate toward watching our games,” Bose says. “Our Thanksgiving viewership has always been strong. I think it’s exploded. We hit 57 million viewers with the Kansas City-Dallas game last year.”

There is precedent for staking out new NFL-watching traditions, as the league has worked closely with Amazon Prime Video over the last few years to successfully establish the Black Friday game on the day after Thanksgiving.

“We’ve been building Black Friday with Amazon over the last few years. So Thanksgiving is a clear natural weekend where people are together,” Bose says. “They’re watching football. We’ve seen that on Christmas also in the last few years. We just looked at it as an opportunity to do something new.”

It’s been a fruitful marriage between the NFL, which has wants to expand the popularity of football around the world, and the streaming giant with its global footprint in 190 countries. For Thanksgiving Eve, NBC Sports is producing the game broadcast; EverWonder Studio is serving as executive producer for Netflix. Jesse Collins Entertainment, the banner known for handling the Emmy Awards, Oscars and Super Bowl Halftime show, is producing U2’s halftime performance.

Netflix’s expanded agreement with the NFL runs through the 2029 season, with a growing presence across the NFL calendar, from Week 1 through Super Bowl week.

This year, Netflix streamed the first-ever NFL game played in Australia, a matchup between the San Francisco 49ers and the Los Angeles Rams, on Sept. 10. The NFL also played games in London, Madrid, Munich, Rio de Janeiro, Paris and Mexico City this year. Live sports are a coveted draw for broadcasters and streamers alike. In the U.S., through the Nov. 22 matchup, the NFL has averaged 18.2 million viewers across its broadcasts, its highest mid-season number since 2010.

Netflix’s partnership with the league includes Christmas Day games, the NFL’s first regular-season game in Australia, the Thanksgiving Eve game, a Week 18 matchup and the annual NFL Honors awards ceremony.

This week’s game marks Netflix’s second time working with the L.A. Rams this season.

“Our experience working with Netflix has been fantastic. There is a shared commitment between the Rams and Netflix to put fans first and deliver an exceptional experience, both inside SoFi Stadium in Inglewood, and for audiences watching around the globe,” says Kathryn Kai-ling Frederick, chief marketing officer for the Rams. “Netflix brings a unique ability to combine world-class storytelling, entertainment and live sports, creating opportunities to reach new audiences and elevate the way fans engage with the game. We’re excited to continue building on our relationship and collaborate on moments that extend beyond the field.”

The strategy with the NFL is to create lasting moments and cement Netflix in the viewers’ minds as a marquee destination for top games and entertainment. “We’re not the networks that have hundreds of games a year. We’re really showing up intentionally at a few points across the season, and I think this is where Thanksgiving Eve fits in perfectly,” Spitzer says. “We’re that opening game, an international game in Melbourne, Thanksgiving Eve, two games on Christmas and then that final weekend of the season when stakes are all on the line for some of these potential playoff teams.”

The Rams and Packers, both with a record of 6-4, are each fighting for a spot in the playoffs, which means that Wednesday’s game has big consequences for both teams’ seasons.

Bose says that the Netflix partnership was a natural for the NFL’s plans, especially after seeing how the streamer brought the spotlight to its Christmas Day games.

“They’ve truly embraced Christmas. They’ve made it into a big event with their double headers. It’s worldwide distribution. They’ve engaged in putting on concerts,” Bose says. “There’s not a lot of places where we can add new windows where we have partners that are well established in certain places. This was a new window. It fits with their strategy of wanting to have kind of events and standalone windows that resonate.”

Spitzer says that Netflix approaches its live events with an eye to setting a high bar in the pop culture landscape. It did that in 2024 with the “Beyoncé Bowl” during the Houston Texans-Baltimore Ravens Christmas Day game, which drew some 27 million viewers in the U.S., not to mention the social media conversation generated by her performance. Beyonce’s revue included Shaboozey, the Texas Southern University’s Oceans of Soul Marching band and a wide range of performers that spoke to Southern Black and Latin history and music.

In 2025, Snoop Dogg’s halftime show for Netflix’s Christmas Day games drew 29 million viewers and featured country superstar Lainey Wilson and the hitmakers of “K-pop Demon Hunters”: EJAE, Audrey Nuna and Rei Ami.

“Obviously, we’re so early in our live sports journey, but it was a great start,” Spitzer says.

With U2, Netflix aims to create another big moment with a band that’s a household name worldwide. “They’ve only done it twice,” Spitzer says about the rock superstars’ NFL game appearances. U2 played in the 2002 game in a moving set that was also a tribute to the victims of 9/11; the other was as an opening act on “Monday Night Football” in 2006 as part of the reopening of the Louisiana Superdome a year after the devastation of Hurricane Katrina. “They want to be a part of a big moment, and we’re thrilled that they want to be on Netflix for launching this new tradition as well,” Spitzer says.

The streamer is also bringing celebrity chef David Chang aboard in the pregame show to spotlight food, tailgating and other game-day traditions. For Netflix, it’s as much of a live event strategy as it is a sports strategy.

“It’s about creating that new fandom. We want to create those global water cooler moments,” says Spitzer. “That could be a big boxing match. It could be NFL games. It could be the Women’s World Cup, or it could be Alex Honnold climbing a 101-story building. If we’re going to do a big live event, we really want to make sure it cuts through.”

Netflix has the rights to the 2027 and 2031 FIFA Women’s World Cup, so lessons learned staging NFL games and other live events look to culminate next year in Brazil. Spitzer won’t comment on whether the streamer is bidding for the 2030 and 2024 Men’s World Cup rights.

The Women’s World Cup is “the most ambitious project that we’ve ever done. Thirty straight days, 64 matches over eight hours a day in the U.S. and Canada,” Spitzer says. “We’ve been planning it for two years. There’s nothing bigger, so we can’t wait for that.”

For Netflix, aligning with the biggest brand in U.S. sports — the NFL — and creating a new tradition is good for driving global subscriptions and good for the bottom line in a business that has seen layoffs and management shake-ups like every other content giant. For the next few weeks, Team Netflix is all about driving viewership to Thanksgiving Eve and Christmas Day action on the gridiron.

“We want to create a new family tradition with Netflix,” Spitzer says. “It’s really about bringing fans together for the biggest moments in sports.”

(Pictured: Beyoncé performing at Netflix’s first Christmas Day telecasts of NFL games in 2024)

Inside WNBA Star Cameron Brink and Ben Felter’s Stanford Wedding—and Her Four Bridal Looks

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

WNBA basketball player Cameron Brink and rower-turned-robotics founder Ben Felter were the rare college students who were able to find love at the height of Covid. Since they were athletes, the Stanford freshmen were in the tiny group allowed on campus during the fall of 2020. “The only people I socialized with were my basketball teammates—until the upperclassmen on my team had us fill out an ‘athlete mingle survey,’” Cameron says.

She begrudgingly filled out the form, was matched with Ben, and later received an email with the subject, “attempting to mingle.” In the message, Ben wrote: “I wanted to chat you up but thought DMs were a bit creepy, so here’s my number.” Cameron—who goes by Cam—says she jumped up and down in her room, elated that he was gentlemanly enough to reach out with his number.

“From there, we had three dates where Ben showed me his hometown of Palo Alto, and he asked me to be his girlfriend five days before we won a national championship,” says Cam. “He is my forever good luck charm.” She adds her friends and teammates Lexie and Lacie Hull were the ones who paired them together and is “forever grateful for their vision.”

Their relationship grew as they graduated college and Cam had begun playing as the Los Angeles Sparks’ forward. On September 30, 2024, Cam was on a trip to Paris for fashion week, and Ben knew it was the perfect opportunity to propose. “I snuck over from California to surprise her with the ring,” he says. “She thought she was going to an important after-party, but really, it was just me waiting for her on the terrace of the Shangri-La Hotel overlooking the Eiffel Tower that was shining with pink lights that night.” At first, Cam was so stunned by the view that she didn’t realize there was no party. “I’ll never forget the expression of pure surprise on her face when it finally sank in that it was me standing in front of her,” remembers Ben. “I told her that it is my purpose to make her happy before getting on one knee and asking her to marry me. She fell to the ground crying and luckily said ‘yes.’”

The couple always knew they wanted to tie the knot at Stanford’s Memorial Church. “Walking past the ornate mosaics on my way to class was always the best part of my day,” says Cam. At first, the bride was nervous that they would have to be on a waiting list for up to four years, but luckily, the university gives alumni a shorter waiting period. The couple set the date for November 14, 2026, with a reception at the Ritz-Carlton, Half Moon Bay, just 30 minutes away.

Cam says she took on the nickname “bridechilla” in her engagement period, attributing her details-focused planner Kristi Lee for making everything so seamless. “Other than working with the church’s clergy, daydreaming with my mom, and scrolling with Ben most nights on Pinterest for inspiration, we were lucky enough to have our family and wedding planner to carry most of the load,” she says. “I guess I always knew it would be beautiful because I have the best circle of humans imaginable that helped the vision come to life.”

Another MVP on the wedding team? “My stylist Shiona Turini was our North Star when it came to what both Ben and I wore,” says Cam. “When she agreed to help me find my dream wedding dress, I never knew it would end with two custom looks from Danielle Frankel, the designer I have been bookmarking since my sophomore year of college.” Cam first tried on styles at Frankel’s Los Angeles salon and found two silhouettes she loved. Frankel herself helped collaborate on customizations. “We ended up perfectly mixing what I envisioned with Danielle’s additions and creating a gown that felt timeless yet extravagant,” the bride explains. For the ceremony, Cam would wear a silk wool deep-V column dress with crinkle organza godets that helped form a full, bubble skirt. Later at the reception, she would change into a column gown crafted with an exaggerated neckline and textural crinkle organza strips. Each gown was accessorized with Manolo Blahnik satin heels and desert diamond studs by Irene Jewels.

Frankel also helped dress the groom for his walk down the aisle with a custom single-breasted tuxedo jacket and bow tie. “The silk lapels were the same fabric as my dress, and it paired together so perfectly,” says Cam. The bow tie is now part of a new menswear offering by the designer. “Ben also wore the watch that I gifted him from our friend Eric Wind—a vintage Rolex Root Beer GMT-Master from the ’70s.”

A trip to New York for fittings coincided with the city’s bridal fashion week, and Cam was invited to see Di Petsa’s first bridal collection. “I knew right away I had to wear the silk long dress with pearl details for my rehearsal dinner,” says the bride. “I adore pearls and wanted jewelry that played off of the pearl details of the back of the Di Petsa gown.” She eventually settled on jewelry from Mateo New York, and also had pearl details from Jennifer Behr added to her hair for the night. The bride also found another Di Petsa look with draped tulle and lace to wear at the after-party. “The discreet pearl at the belly button was so special,” Cam says. “We paired it with one of my favorite shoe designers—Amina Muaddi’s lace heels. But to be honest, I kicked off the shoes at the end of the night and ended up in a pair of New Balance sneakers!”

To finish off every look with the right glam, Cam had her makeup artist Jocelyn Biga make a trip from New York for the celebration. “The day wouldn’t have felt complete without her as she’s done my makeup for all of the biggest events in my life: the WNBA draft, our engagement, and now the wedding,” says the bride.

The wedding party was also outfitted for the big day. Amsale made custom dresses in deep blue for the bridesmaids, and groomsmen wore classic tuxedos. “For our entire group of children, Maison Ava created a bespoke wardrobe,” explains Cam. “The girls' looks had hand embroidery, hand-cut and hand-appliquéd floral motifs, crystals, beading, and 3D flowers, [while] the boys had custom velvet tuxedo jackets and wool trousers. It was so incredible to see all of the kids in their looks, and they also had tiny, custom New Balance sneakers at the end of the night!”

After a morning getting ready with their closest circles and taking portraits along the California coastline, the couple reunited at their alma mater to exchange vows at the Memorial Church. “The light was shining through the stained-glass windows so brightly all I could see was Cameron’s beautiful silhouette as she walked down the aisle with her parents, Greg and Michelle, and I tried to hold back tears,” remembers Ben. Cam admits she tripped right before walking down the aisle (and thankfully, her mom came to the rescue), but all her nerves washed away once Ben lifted her veil. “I felt overwhelmed by the support from our loved ones, and most of all I felt the deepest sense of gratitude and belonging looking into Ben’s eyes,” she says. The bride’s godmother read scripture, the couple exchanged personal vows, and their pastor gave wishes for their future, including “a peaceful home filled with joy, laughter, and one day, the sound of little feet running around wearing number 22.”

Once the newlyweds paraded down the aisle and did the dip they practiced all week, they sped off to the Ritz-Carlton, Half Moon Bay in a 1957 Rolls-Royce. “I asked our driver for his best marriage advice since he’s been with his wife for 58 years,” recalls Ben. “He said, ‘It’s not that complicated. I just do what she says.’ Cameron elbowed me and got a kick out of that.” After portraits at sunset, the bride and groom joined their guests for cocktail hour on the grounds.

“The rest of the night was filled with so much joy, belly laughs, and dorky dance moves,” says Ben. The reception moved indoors to a space filled with florals and greenery curated with the help of Flowers by Edgar. Dinner kicked off with the couple’s first dance to “Until I Found You,” sung by their flower girl Ryan. The night was also peppered with speeches from the bride’s father and the groom’s brothers. As the dance floor lit up, guests could also head outdoors to indulge in a cigar lounge. “The energy in the ballroom was unmatched, erupting multiple times with cheers and toasts,” adds Ben. “We couldn’t be happier with how the best day of our lives concluded.”

Now off on their honeymoon in Kyoto, the pair share they are in a bubble of newlywed bliss. Says Cam: “We couldn’t have dreamt of a better day and are feeling so incredibly blessed to have shared it with all of the people whom we love so dearly.”

Our favorite part of the invitations was the custom watercolor art of the Stanford Memorial Church by Shannon of Luxe Marie Stationery.

Opinion: Liverpool need to steal wonderkid from Premier League rival

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Liverpool will be in the market for a new midfielder in the upcoming transfer windows.

Andoni Iraola has fixed several issues at Anfield this season, but the Spaniard has still struggled to find balance in the midfield.

Florian Wirtz is a must-start, but the double pivot behind him has been chopped and changed and it feels as though a fresh face may now be the best solution.

Multiple top midfielders have been linked with Merseyside moves, but there is one wonderkid that the Reds might be about to overlook.

Liverpool aren't the only team looking for reinforcements, and they must be smart to ensure they get the best available players.

Liverpool need to be looking at Premier League wonderkidOne of the most exciting names in the Premier League a couple of seasons ago was Archie Gray. When the Englishman moved from Leeds to Tottenham, many thought he was nailed on to become a full England international, but the 20-year-old's career has stalled. Tottenham's recent struggles may have contributed to Gray's slow progress, and a fresh challenge could reignite the wonderkid's career.

One of the most exciting names in the Premier League a couple of seasons ago was Archie Gray.

When the Englishman moved from Leeds to Tottenham, many thought he was nailed on to become a full England international, but the 20-year-old's career has stalled.

Tottenham's recent struggles may have contributed to Gray's slow progress, and a fresh challenge could reignite the wonderkid's career.

According to Sky Sports' Lyall Thomas, Brentford are monitoring the 20-year-old's situation ahead of the January window after showing interest in the summer.

Gray has played regularly for Spurs this season but only played one minute in Saturday's victory over Ipswich after being dropped to the bench.

Roberto De Zerbi's side are still some way off where they want to be, and cashing in on Gray this winter could be a tempting offer for them.

The Reds hold a historic interest in the midfielder, who has played mostly as a right-back this season, and have shown a clear pathway to first-team football for several other young talents who have made Merseyside moves in recent years.

If Gray does become available this winter Julian Ward should definitely take note, and the 20-year-old could be given the chance to relaunch his career in L4.

Wash Full Loads on Cold – Heating Water Is About 90% of a Washer’s Energy

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Heating the water takes most of the energy your washing machine uses, so set everyday loads to cold and run the washer only when it’s full. Save hot water for the few loads that need it to kill germs or cut grease.

The washer’s motor is the cheap part of a load. Energy Star puts water heating at about 90% of a clothes washer’s energy use.

Take a standard washer, which uses about 20 gallons a load by Energy Star’s count, and assume a hot wash heats half of that, 10 gallons, from 50 to 120 degrees. With an electric water heater, that takes about 1.7 kilowatt-hours, roughly 30 cents at 17.23 cents per kilowatt-hour, the 2025 average residential price from the Energy Information Administration.

A gas water heater that gets about 80% of its fuel’s heat into the water does the same job for about 11 cents, at the agency’s 2025 residential average of $15.34 per thousand cubic feet. Switching from hot to warm can cut either cost in half, Energy Star says, and the cold cycle cuts the cost further. An Energy Star front-loader uses about half the water of a top-loader with an agitator, so switching it to cold saves less.

Hot water is still worth paying for on a few loads. During a stomach bug, the CDC says to wash clothes, towels, or bedding with vomit or stool on them in hot water on the longest cycle, then machine dry them on the highest heat. Greasy work clothes are the other case, since Energy Star’s advice to wash cold makes an exception for oily stains. Wash everything else on cold, with a detergent made for cold water, as the South Carolina Energy Office advises.

Run fewer, fuller loads, too, and start them at night or on weekends if your utility charges less at those times. A washer uses about the same energy whatever the size of the load, Energy Star says, so two half loads cost about twice what one full load does. If a small load can’t wait, set the water level to match it. For the rest of the electric bill, start with heating, cooling, and hot water.

A family that switches eight warm loads a week to cold saves up to $61 a year with an electric water heater, or $22 with gas.

SFA could open ‘huge can of worms’ with ‘two-game’ Hyun-jun Yang ban after Celtic scenes – Keith Wyness

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Celtic winger Hyun-jun Yang could potentially face a “two-game” ban after he appeared to dive to win a free-kick during last weekend’s 4-1 win over St Mirren.

That is according to former Aberdeen chief executive Keith Wyness, speaking exclusively to Football Insider, who believes that the South Korean could potentially face retrospective punishment.

Yang went down midway through the St Mirren half in the 32nd minute, with referee Matthew MacDermid awarding a free-kick, which Alex Oxlade-Chamberlain eventually scored the opening goal from.

However, Wyness believes that the SFA must set a new precedent of banning players for dives outside of penalty incidents if they are to punish Yang.

Fans on social media have been in uproar over the incident, while former Premier League referee Dermot Gallagher has pointed out that VAR couldn’t intervene as it wasn’t a penalty incident.

Yang’s incident has prompted plenty of debate in recent days, but Wyness is sceptical as to whether the 24-year-old will face any punishment.

Aberdeen’s former chief Wyness – who served as CEO at Pittodrie between 2000 and 2004 and now runs a consultancy business advising elite clubs – believes that the SFA would be opening a “huge can of worms” if they handed a ban to Yang.

The Scottish businessman pointed out that it would then set a precedent for dives to be reviewed all over the pitch, not just in the penalty area.

Speaking on the latest edition of Football Insider’s Inside Track podcast, Wyness explained that it is not a straightforward decision as to whether Yang should face punishment.

“Well, this is probably a little bit more interesting in terms of the situation here because there have been retrospective actions on penalties, but this was for a free kick,” he said.

“He could see up to a two-game ban. But what that would mean is opening up a whole new situation of simulation for free kicks, which could be all over the pitch as opposed to penalty kicks.

“Now that's a big difference. And so we'll see how they actually handle that one. It may not be an area they want to go into in terms of opening up simulation for free kicks everywhere because that does open a huge can of worms.

“Penalty kicks I understand but I think this may be just too big a bridge for them to travel on this one.”

Wyness recently told Football Insider that Celtic fans will be unhappy to see the club exploit their “glorious past” with their latest release of watches ahead of the Christmas period.

The Bhoys are selling special edition Lisbon Lions watches at £299, with only 1,967 set to be sold, the year of their Champions League triumph.

However, Wyness only expects the club to make around “£100,000” in profit from the new scheme.

He also feels that Celtic may have handed their fanbase another stick to beat them with, as the discontent at Parkhead continues.
