Stefan Borson: Man City's owners could still agree '£1.5bn' deal with US firm

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Manchester City's owners could return to the table with Apollo Global Management over a potential $2billion (£1.5billion) loan deal.

That is according to former Man City financial adviser Stefan Borson, who exclusively told Football Insider that the US private capital group are likely still doing business with the owners behind the scenes.

The Citizens' owners were in line to take on another £1.5billion in debt, but saw the deal come crashing down after 114 charges landed at the Etihad.

Of course, Man City have appealed the guilty verdict, with fans up and down the country, and even across Europe, waiting for the result.

The guilty verdict forced Apollo to pull out of the loan, which was always likely under such circumstances, but a return to the table wouldn't be a surprise.

Should City be able to mitigate some of the charges or get them dropped altogether, the owners should be able to restart talks over the big-money loan.

Speaking exclusively to Football Insider, Borson believes that removal of "more toxic findings" could get Man City owners' plans back on track.

"That might change if City, obviously, win the appeal or remove some of the more toxic findings of the independent commission," he said.

"Then I suspect they'll go back to the drawing board with Apollo and probably will be able to do a deal. But in the context of what had just been published around the end of October...

"There's no possibility that a credit committee would say, 'Yeah, we'll just play on, go and lend them $2billion,' because, ultimately, the whole credit risk of the situation has changed now.

"That's not to say they're not doing business with Abu Dhabi, but as the City Football Group, the credit risk has changed, largely because of the uncertainty, but also because the findings of the independent commission so far are around sham contracts.

"We know that's what they found and that they misled the auditors so the numbers are wrong. So, against that backdrop, as I say, no credit committee is going to say, 'Yeah, just carry on'."

Fans will definitely be questioning why the Man City owners need an injection of cash when all the controversy is surrounding their biggest club's books.

Borson has explained the reality of the City Football Group, and why the owners are not actually profitable despite all the success.

He said: "It's a lot money, clearly, and the burn of the organization is big. But they do have a lot of clubs, and they do have a lot of bills to pay.

"It's partly because they're building the stadium in New York. And clearly, none of the companies are profitable, because football clubs are very rarely profitable."

GLP-1s Like Ozempic May Boost Brain Health, But Can They Lower Dementia Risk?

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

GLP-1 medications have transformed obesity and type 2 diabetes treatment. Now, researchers are investigating whether the effects of this blockbuster class of drugs extend beyond weight loss and affect brain health.

A comprehensive new review published on November 5 in the journal Science evaluated the current evidence on how GLP-1 medications like semaglutide and tirzepatide may affect the brain across aging, cardiometabolic disease, and Alzheimer’s disease.

The authors of the review suggested that GLP-1 medications like Ozempic and Zepbound may offer brain health benefits. However, they were also left with the question of whether the medications act directly on the brain or whether better brain health is actually a consequence of improved cardiometabolic health.

Sheena Aurora, MD, vice president, Medical Affairs, Alzheimer’s Association, who wasn’t involved in the review, told Healthline that more research is needed to better understand the effects of GLP-1 drugs on the brain.

“While research suggests GLP-1s could reduce brain inflammation, improve blood vessel health, and even protect neurons, much broader, randomized clinical trials on GLP-1s and brain health, including in people with Alzheimer’s disease and other dementias, are needed,” she said.

GLP-1 medications like semaglutide, the active ingredient in Ozempic and Wegovy, mimic the GLP-1 hormone. Tirzepatide, the active ingredient in Mounjaro and Zepbound, also targets a second hormone pathway called GIP.

These medications have been shown to produce significant weight loss and improvements in cardiometabolic health.

This is important for brain health because obesity, type 2 diabetes, and vascular dysfunction are all linked with cognitive impairment and dementia. Insulin resistance and chronic inflammation may also link metabolic and brain health.

Analyses of large outcome trials and observational cohorts have suggested that GLP-1 medications may be associated with a reduced risk of cognitive impairment and dementia. However, randomized trials in diseases like Alzheimer’s disease have yet to demonstrate clinical benefits.

Around 200 preclinical and mechanistic studies have shown benefits for learning, memory, synaptic plasticity, and neuronal resilience, though results are heterogeneous in design and direction, so a clear consensus on magnitude and mechanism remains lacking.

It is also unclear how well GLP-1 medications reach the brain. They appear to reach certain areas, such as the hypothalamus, brainstem, and circumventricular organs, more readily than regions protected by the blood-brain barrier.

The report authors noted that some studies have shown that GLP-1 receptor agonists may reduce the risk of cognitive impairment and dementia in people with type 2 diabetes. This may be due to cognitive issues being linked to factors such as blood sugar, blood pressure, vascular health, and sleep.

Mir Ali, MD, medical director of MemorialCare Surgical Weight Loss Center at Orange Coast Medical Center in Fountain Valley, CA, who wasn’t involved in the report, told Healthline, “These findings are interesting, but like many of the other benefits associated with GLP-1 medications, a direct causal relationship has not yet been proven.”

“Rather, the benefits appear to be more closely tied to the reduction in obesity and better control of diabetes,” he said.

Aurora noted that growing evidence supports the heart-brain connection.

She cited new findings from the Alzheimer’s Association’s landmark U.S. POINTER study presented at the Clinical Trials in Alzheimer’s Disease (CTAD) conference November 16–19 in Boston, MA.

The research showed that the structured lifestyle intervention program also improved heart health risk factors, such as:

“U.S. POINTER can change those risk factors within 24 months, and better heart health risk-factor control was linked with better cognition,” Aurora said. “We’re also interested to see whether adding a drug like a GLP-1 to a U.S. POINTER-style lifestyle program improves cognition in at-risk older adults more than the program alone.”

To find out, the Alzheimer’s Association is funding the Prevention of Risk for Cognitive Decline through Combined Therapy (PROTECT-Cog) global phase 3 clinical trial.

PROTECT-Cog is a first-of-its-kind trial designed to evaluate whether combining a proven multidomain lifestyle intervention with a metabolism-targeting drug, like a GLP-1, can further reduce the risk of cognitive decline, mild cognitive impairment, and dementia in at-risk older adults.

Dung Trinh, MD, internist at MemorialCare Medical Group and Chief Medical Officer of Healthy Brain Clinic in Irvine, CA, who also wasn’t involved in the review, told Healthline, “I often remind my patients that what’s good for the heart is generally good for the brain.”

“The brain depends on a healthy cardiovascular system to deliver a constant supply of oxygen, glucose, and other nutrients. Conditions such as high blood pressure, diabetes, obesity, and high cholesterol can damage blood vessels, promote chronic inflammation, and increase the risk of stroke and vascular cognitive impairment,” he said.

“Over time, these changes can also contribute to the development or progression of dementia.”

Trinh added that while GLP-1 medications can improve several cardiometabolic risk factors, these improvements do not automatically translate into proven dementia prevention.

“Regular physical activity, a heart-healthy diet, adequate sleep, and management of cardiovascular risk factors remain essential strategies for maintaining brain health as we age,” he said.

When the report authors turned their attention to more stringent tests on semaglutide in people already living with early Alzheimer’s disease, the picture began to change.

Novo Nordisk’s EVOKE and EVOKE + Phase 3 Trials examined the efficacy of semaglutide in people with early stage Alzheimer’s disease.

The studies showed that while semaglutide produced changes in several disease-related biomarkers, it didn’t slow the progression of Alzheimer’s disease.

This emphasizes that fixing metabolic issues might prevent cognitive decline in someone with obesity or diabetes. But it might not be enough to reverse the damage in someone with established Alzheimer’s disease, where profound synaptic loss and protein pathology have already occurred.

The review authors suggest that future research on obesity and diabetes should also include rigorous and specific cognitive testing.

‘Mindhunter’ Officially Meets ‘True Detective’ in Netflix’s New 4-Part Crime Thriller

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Hannah covers horror, sci-fi, and games at Collider, where she writes news and features, reviews new releases, and interviews the people who make them. She’s helping build out Collider’s gaming coverage, with Pokémon as one of her specialties. If she isn’t writing about a monster, she’s probably watching one on a tape from her VHS collection.

Anna Bersz has held two jobs that give her good reason to remember the name Mariusz Trynkiewicz. In 1988, she was the investigator looking for the man who murdered four boys in Poland. By 2014, she’s a prison director, and the date everyone wants her to answer for is the one when the murderer's sentence runs out. There’s someone Anna could call who knows the case as well as she does, but they haven’t been friends since the first time they worked the case.

The series follows Anna and Agnieszka Grabska across two stages of the Trynkiewicz case. In 1988, the women worked together while Trynkiewicz was still a suspect, and Agnieszka, a psychiatrist, was asked to assess his mental capacity. Decades later, with his release approaching, she’s called back to evaluate him again. This time, the question is whether he poses a danger to the public.

All four episodes of The Law of Beasts are now streaming on Netflix. The Polish crime thriller premiered November 18, with Agnieszka Grochowska as Anna, Agnieszka Żulewska as Agnieszka, and Michał Pawlik as Trynkiewicz. Kasper Bajon directed the series and wrote it with Anna Kasińska. At roughly three hours altogether, it’s a weekend watch you can finish in an evening.

A great detective is really just a personality with a method attached, which is why we keep remaking the same four of them. Here is yours, what it says about you, and the case for them being the best in the business.

You let people think you are not a threat and then you simply never go away. Columbo is the only major detective show that tells you who did it in the first ten minutes, because the pleasure was never the puzzle, it was watching a rich man slowly realise he is in trouble.

You are the most interesting person in the room and you know exactly how to use it. Rian Johnson built Blanc as a gentleman detective who is also an outsider everywhere he goes, which lets him be charming at people who would never otherwise talk to him.

Everybody tells you everything, and you have never once asked them to. Jessica Fletcher is a crime novelist from a small town in Maine who is so warm and so obviously trustworthy that suspects confess to her essentially as a social courtesy.

You expect to be lied to and you are almost never disappointed. Chandler wrote Marlowe as a man with a rigid private code in a city that has none, which is why he takes the case, solves it, gets nothing out of it and takes the next one.

Mariusz Trynkiewicz was a real man convicted of murdering four boys in the Polish city of Piotrków Trybunalski in 1988. A court sentenced him to death the following year. Under a 1989 amnesty, that sentence became 25 years in prison. As his release approached in 2014, Poland adopted legislation known as the “Beasts Act,” which allowed courts to order some offenders into secure treatment after they had served their sentences. The regulations took effect so close to Trynkiewicz’s release that the court was still considering his case when he left prison.

Anna and Agnieszka are fictional. Bajon said he and Kasińska created them after learning how many women had been responsible for decisions in the real case. “The men talked at length about how to solve the problem, but the responsibility ultimately often fell to the women,” he told Netflix. The writers drew on several people they met during their research to create the two leads. Their friendship gave the series a way to follow both the original investigation and the fight over Trynkiewicz’s release from inside the institutions making those calls.

The Law of Beasts is streaming on Netflix.

Prediction: Some Anthropic Investors Are About to Learn an Expensive Lesson

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Anthropic broke records this week, but some investors may be setting themselves up for disappointment.

By Katie Brockman – Nov 21, 2026 at 7:41PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Anthropic ›

Anthropic (ANT -8.50%) made history this week with the largest stock market debut on record, securing $81 billion in funding and surpassing SpaceX's initial public offering (IPO) earlier this year.

There was no shortage of investor enthusiasm either, as the stock popped by more than 17% during its first day of trading. While nobody can say how the stock will fare in the coming days or weeks, I predict that some Anthropic investors are about to learn a hard, expensive lesson.

Dario Amodei. Image source: Getty Images.

Blockbuster IPOs are often turbulent straight out of the gate, experiencing extreme price swings in their initial weeks and months.

SpaceX, for example, has fallen by more than 20% from its initial price, as of this writing. Semiconductor breakout star SK Hynix has plunged by nearly 30% since it debuted in July, and Meta Platforms – then known as Facebook – sank by more than 50% in its first three months of trading in 2012.

In Meta's case, the stock eventually bounced back, delivering total returns of around 1,400% since its IPO. But the investors who reaped those rewards were those who stayed invested through all the ups and downs rather than expecting immediate riches.

The same thing could be said for Anthropic. Over time, it could be a lucrative investment. The company recently generated $17 billion in third-quarter revenue, significantly outpacing its $1.4 billion figure from 2025.

But Anthropic also has some major bills to keep up with. Its IPO prospectus disclosed over $900 billion in expense commitments and a $104 billion net operating loss in 2026, and time will tell whether the company's revenue is sufficient to fund the enormous computing power its technology demands.

This isn't to say that Anthropic is a bad investment. But as with any IPO, there are a lot of unknowns right now, and anything could happen in the coming weeks and months.

If you believe in the business model and are willing to hold your investment for at least a few years despite any potential short-term volatility, it could be a smart buy – assuming it's part of a well-diversified portfolio.

However, investors who are buying primarily because of the hype around the stock, hoping to make a quick buck, have the most to lose. The short term is likely to be a bumpy ride, and those who are only buying with the hope of earning a fortune overnight could end up sorely disappointed.

Fundamentals beat hype every time, and the strongest investments are those with solid underlying business fundamentals and long-term growth potential.

Find Small Business Grants Close to Home – Not in Washington

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Look for small business grants close to home, from city and state economic development offices, utilities, corporations, and foundations. The SBA gives no grants to start or expand a business, and federal grants for companies mostly fund research or exporting. Apply only where your business fits the grant’s purpose.

A grant is the cheapest money a business can get, since you never repay it and no one takes a share of the company.

The U.S. Small Business Administration “does not provide grants for starting and expanding a business,” according to its own grants page. The federal grants a company can win go mostly to research, through the Small Business Innovation Research (SBIR) and Small Business Technology Transfer (STTR) programs, and to exporters.

So for a shop, a restaurant, or a service business, the grants worth chasing are local.

Take a bakery owner who searches the federal grant listings and finds research and export programs, nothing for a bakery. Her city’s economic development office runs a storefront program that pays back half the cost of exterior work, up to $10,000. She applies before hiring anyone, as the program requires, wins a grant, spends $16,000 on a new sign, awning, and lighting, and gets $8,000 back once the work is done.

Here’s where to look, starting close to home, with what each source pays for and who can apply.

A real government grant never charges you a fee. The Federal Trade Commission says no agency will reach out to demand that you pay for a grant, and it warns against paying any up-front fee.

It also warns against paying for a list of grants, since the complete list of federal grants on Grants.gov is free.

Check the sender, too. The SBA says it writes only from email addresses ending in @sba.gov, and that anyone claiming to be the agency from another address should be reported as fraud.

Read what the grant pays for first, since many fund a single purpose. Philadelphia’s Storefront Improvement Program covers exterior work such as signs, awnings, lighting, and paint, and STEP money goes only to export activities the SBA approves.

Then check what you’d have to put in yourself. Philadelphia’s program pays back up to half the cost of approved work, so you cover at least half and pay the whole bill until the city reimburses you. USDA’s Value-Added Producer Grants require a dollar-for-dollar match.

Many grants also want a business that’s already running. STEP is for established small businesses, and the $25,000 Wells Fargo grants the Manhattan Chamber of Commerce offered in 2026 required two years in business.

The competition is steep. Boston’s storefront program says it lacks the money to give a grant to every eligible business, and Hiring Our Heroes names five winners a round. Winning takes an application that answers the funder’s scoring criteria point by point.

Start with one call to your city’s economic development office, and ask which grants it’s funding this year, what each one pays for, and how much of the cost you’d have to cover.

15 Cities Where Rent Still Covers a Rental’s Mortgage – Then Check Taxes & Insurance

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Buy your next rental where rent is high against home prices. In 15 Midwest and Southern metros, a typical house rents for 7.4% to 11.2% of its value a year. Before you offer, check the city’s taxes, insurance, and vacancy, and what houses on that street actually rent for.

That share, a year’s rent divided by the price, is the gross yield, and it decides whether rent can cover a mortgage. In Zillow’s October 2026 figures, a typical house in the San Diego area rents for about 5% of its value a year, while one in the McAllen, Texas, area rents for 11.2%.

Now figure the mortgage payment on each, with 25% down and a 30-year loan at 7%. A typical McAllen-area house, worth $189,000, rents for about $1,760 a month, roughly $820 more than its $943 payment. A typical San Diego-area house, worth about $1 million, rents for about $4,130, about $860 short of its payment of nearly $5,000.

At those terms, the typical rent is higher than the mortgage payment in all 15 metros below, by about $270 a month in Little Rock up to about $820 in McAllen. That margin still has to pay the property taxes and insurance, plus the repairs, empty months, and management every landlord budgets for, so those costs decide whether a house makes money.

Since October 2023, typical rents have risen faster than typical home prices, so the yield on a typical U.S. house has climbed from 7.0% to 7.4%.

Each price, rent, and yield here covers a whole metro area and uses Zillow’s typical single-family home value and rent, the middle of each market. Every metro on the list passed six tests:

Two big Texas metros fell short. San Antonio’s typical rent rose only 0.7% over the past year, and Census’s metro estimates put Dallas’s rental vacancy at 14.7% this summer, nearly twice the national rate.

Here they are, from the highest yield down, with each metro’s typical house value and monthly rent.

Property taxes come out of the rent every year, and several states on this list tax a rental more heavily than an owner’s home. Texas’s homestead exemptions, which shield part of a home’s value from tax, go only to an owner’s main home, so a rental in McAllen or El Paso is taxed on its full value, the state comptroller explains. Michigan charges a rental up to 18 mills, or $18 per $1,000 of taxable value, in school operating taxes that an owner’s home skips, according to its Treasury Department.

In South Carolina, a rental is assessed at 6% of its value, against 4% for an owner’s home, and pays school operating taxes the owner’s home skips, as Richland County, home to Columbia, explains. Indiana caps a rental’s yearly property tax at 2% of its assessed value, against 1% for an owner’s home, according to the state’s Department of Local Government Finance.

Insurance is the other big cost that varies by state. In Insurance.com’s 2026 study of quoted rates for a standard policy with $300,000 of coverage on the house, Nebraska, Oklahoma, and Kansas averaged more than $5,000 a year, about twice the $2,872 national average. Texas averaged $4,582. Get a quote on a landlord policy for the exact house before you offer.

Landlord law matters as well. Oregon, Washington, and California cap yearly rent increases statewide, while 36 states bar their cities from adopting rent control at all, according to the National Apartment Association, a landlords’ trade group.

Every number above describes a whole metro, which mixes blocks where houses rent quickly with blocks where they sit empty. Before you offer, compare the rent the seller quotes with what similar houses nearby actually rent for. If you’re buying in another state, have a local agent or property manager pull those rents for you.

Start with the two or three metros whose typical prices fit your budget, and run one real listing in each through the mortgage test above, using its own tax bill and insurance quote.

MU will have to pay a fortune in the United States to recruit Pochettino

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Manchester United will have to put their hands in their pockets if they wish to recruit Mauricio Pochettino. While the future of Michael Carrick on the bench of the Red Devils seems more and more compromised, the United States coach appears to be the big favorite of the Glazer family to succeed him. But according to information from The i Paperthe Mancunian club will have to pay 9 million euros to release the Argentine coach from his contract with the American selection. A significant sum for a 54-year-old technician who recently signed a new four-year contract. Manchester United leaders are due to meet this Wednesday in Monaco to make a decision regarding Michael Carrick, whose departure could be announced as early as Thursday. With only one victory in the first ten days of the Premier League, a first in 53 years for the club, the former midfielder would not have convinced his management to give him more time. Already approached by Manchester United in 2018 and 2022, Pochettino finally saw Ole Gunnar Solskjaer and Erik ten Hag being preferred to him. This time, Joel and Avram Glazer would be determined to make the Argentinian their priority.

However, it remains to convince Mauricio Pochettino to leave the United States, where he seems fully invested in his project. The former Tottenham and Chelsea coach is said to be happy with his current situation, but would agree to listen to a possible Mancunian offer. No official approach has yet been made, and several people close to the matter doubt his desire to join Manchester United in such a complicated sporting context. The Glazers could nevertheless benefit from their relationship with American billionaire Ken Griffin, who helped finance Pochettino’s contract with the American selection and maintains historic financial ties with the owners of the Red Devils. At the same time, two other candidates remain seriously considered by management. Eddie Howe, former Newcastle manager, is said to be the favorite of director of football Jason Wilcox, while Julian Nagelsmann, former Germany manager, is said to be favored by head of recruitment Christopher Vivell. The choice of the future coach could thus depend as much on financial negotiations as on Manchester United’s ability to convince its priority candidate.
